- MEASUREMENT: capacity bounds. 15,589 tokens succeeds at **56.31 GiB — 88 % of the host**; ~73k aborts. Metal's single-buffer limit alone caps n at 29,491; the host binds first, around 17–18k by a two-point extrapolation (labelled as such in the report).
- MEASUREMENT: prefill is the other hard blocker. 8k prompt — Swift 75.5 tok/s vs Python 109.4 (**0.69x**, threshold 0.90), TTFT 103.0 s vs 71.5 s (**1.44x**, threshold 1.10). Decode is a genuine Swift win (1.03x short, 1.02x at 8k) and so is short-prompt TTFT (0.75x), but none of it survives the abort. Peak footprint 1.094x — inside the 1.10 band with a 6 % margin, and 44.01 GiB vs 31.47 GiB at an 8k prompt.
- MEASUREMENT: the teardown-abandonment risk, finally sized on the real model instead of the 261 MB fixture. Provoked with the real fault seam: outcome **abandoned** after **9.988 s**, with **29,744,368,205 B — 27.70 GiB, 43 % of the host — left in MLX's pool** until the supervisor replaces the process. The weights genuinely came back (0 of 1229 live `Module` owners, container deallocated, `returned_bytes` 29,684,985,407 clearing the 29,501,612,496 footprint); the refusal is on **7,984 bytes** of post-generation sampler/RNG state against a zero allowance. Frequency under benchmark load: **0 of 27 generations** — and the one real failure that did occur bypassed the path entirely by trapping.
- FINDING: **the incumbent's prompt cache buys nothing on this model**, which removes the prototype's headline gap. Deployed with `--prompt-cache-size 1 --prompt-cache-bytes 8GB`, the 3-turn shared-prefix scenario re-prefilled in full every turn (0/7822, 0/7851, 0/7880) with the cache populated at 1 sequence / 0.67 GB. Cause in source: `fetch_nearest_cache` can only reuse a longer entry by trimming, gated on `can_trim_prompt_cache`; `qwen3_5` uses `ArraysCache` for 48 of its 64 layers and `ArraysCache` inherits `_BaseCache.is_trimmable() == False`. A linear-attention state cannot be rewound to a prefix.
- FINDING: **`model-harness stress` benchmarks the wrong model against the Python profile.** `mlx_lm.server.handle_models_request` lists every MLX model in the local HF cache and appends the configured `--model` **last**; `waitForStressModel` in `internal/modelharness/stress.go` takes `document.Data[0].ID`. On this host that resolved to `mlx-community/Qwen1.5-0.5B-Chat-4bit`, which the server then downloaded from Hugging Face and served. Any prior `stress` figure for `profiles.qwen-local` is suspect.
- FINDING: the **deployed** Python runtime still has `BUG-260827-1jhv2g`. `/health` writes a literal `{"status": "ok"}` unconditionally; the `_generation_thread.is_alive()` tie exists only in the fork's uninstalled `HEAD` (`b0a45b8`, not an ancestor of the installed `9150698`). Worth installing independently of this decision.
- FINDING: Python answers `/v1/models` 200 at 1.07 s with no weights resident — it loads lazily on the first completion. Readiness there does not mean loadable. The Swift runtime's 503-until-resident contract is a real parity win it does not get to keep.
- EVIDENCE: `swift test -c release` 228 tests / 21 suites exit 0; `xcodebuild` Release exit 0; `swift-format lint --strict` exit 0; `shellcheck -S warning` exit 0; `lifecycle-smoke.sh` 17 checks exit 0; `git diff --check` exit 0. Seven contract mutants against the comparison gate all caught, three of them **narrowing** (pin walk drops one field; overlap refusal relaxed to > 60 s; verdict tolerates one blocker), tree restored to its pristine SHA-256.
- EVIDENCE: production mutant **P1** launders the candidate's pins through the baseline's inside `BenchmarkCompareCommand.run(arguments:)` before calling the gate — the bypass-path shape. Rebuilt with `xcodebuild` and run on a real pair whose `modelDigest` differs: pristine exit **4** and no report; P1 exit **3** with a full decision for two different models; restored exit **4**. The refusal is bound to the entry point, not to a helper.
- STATUS: `TASK-260827-2v13w8` handed to review. Re-open the migration only when chunked prefill lands in `Qwen35VLModel.prepare` and the runtime wraps generation in `withErrorHandler`; re-measure prefill then rather than assuming it.
- SUPERSEDED by the 1712 entry above. The blocker was reachable in this repository through `MLXLLM.LLMModelFactory`, which this entry never exercised, and the prefill figures here were taken at unequal chunk sizes.

### 0530 — A Wrapper Is Not Its Contents
- CORRECTION: the 0330 entry above is wrong where it says the barrier's `weak` reference reading `nil` is "ARC having destroyed *this* worker's weights" and that there is "no threshold and no intermediate state: the object is alive or it has been destroyed". There is an intermediate state, review found it, and the claim is retracted here.
- REGRESSION: `ModelContainer` is a wrapper. The weights live two levels below it — `SerialAccessContainer<ModelContext>` → `ModelContext.model` → the `LanguageModel` and its arrays — and a Swift `weak` reference may read `nil` while destruction of that stored state is still in progress. Review's probe observed the order directly: `payload-deinit-start`, `weak-nil`, `payload-deinit-finish`.
- ROOT CAUSE: the **capability claim that does not reproduce** shape. Wrapper destruction was reported as completed weight release. Revision 3 had replaced a bad aggregate with a good-looking proxy, and a proxy scoped to one object is still a proxy.
- EVIDENCE: review narrowed the pinned dependency — three seconds of delay in `SerialAccessContainer<ModelContext>` destruction, nothing else touched. Every phase before condemned teardown stayed green, including both KV leak checks; phase 6a then emitted `release_observed=true shared_cache_rebuilds=1 pending=false cache_bytes=0` with `active_bytes=262361760`. The runtime cleared the pool and attested a rebuild with the entire model resident.
- FIX: the release verdict is now conjunctive and allocator-bound. The `weak` reference is kept as a **veto** — while the wrapper lives the weights are certainly held — and the attestation comes from MLX's own accounting: at least `LoadedModel.weightFootprintBytes`, the `activeMemory` delta measured across *this* model's load, must have stopped being active since the teardown began. `GenerationBatchRecovery.weightsReleased(_:)` is the pure gate; the barrier only measures.
- FINDING: no slack is needed, and none is used. Whatever is legitimately still active at the end was already active at the baseline, so it cancels out of the subtraction — measured: baseline 302,277,676 B, footprint 262,361,760 B, returned 302,274,956 B, residue 2,720 B of sampler state. An unmeasured footprint (`0`) fails closed rather than admitting every reading, because a measurement that did not happen is not a model that cost nothing.
- FINDING: the existing `--fault-inject-teardown-retain` seam could not have caught this. It parks the *outer* container, so `weak`-`nil` never happens and a wrapper-only barrier refuses for the wrong reason. The new `--fault-inject-teardown-retain-weights` parks `ModelContext.model` instead and lets the container die on schedule, reproducing the disputed interval in production rather than simulating it.
- EVIDENCE: separation on MLX's own figures — released: `container_deallocated=true`, returned 302,274,956 B of a 262,361,760 B footprint, `active_bytes=2,720`, `cache_bytes=0`, rebuilds 1. Inner-retained: `container_deallocated=true` as well, returned only 39,391,628 B, `active_bytes=262,361,760`, rebuilds 0, abandoned 1, pending `true`, `/health` 503.
- EVIDENCE: four mutants die. Production P1 — revision 3's wrapper-only barrier — fails 7 checks and reddens **only** phase 6d, at `shared_cache_rebuilds=1` beside `active_bytes=262,361,760`, the reviewer's defect measured directly. Production N1, **narrowing**: relaxing the byte test to "any release counts" also fails 6d, so the bound is proven by narrowing and not only by deletion. Contract N2: dropping the unmeasured-footprint guard reddens the fail-closed test. Reviewer mutant M8 (delete `Memory.clearCache()`) still dies after the restructure, 2 failures.
- EVIDENCE: 115 checks exit 0; contract suite 182 tests / 19 suites exit 0; `dead-generation-smoke.sh` still 45 checks exit 0; `xcodebuild` Release, `swift-format lint` and `shellcheck -S warning` all exit 0.
- STATUS: `TASK-260827-2q77g8` revision 4 handed back to review.

### 0330 — A Failure To Observe Is Not An Observation Of Success
- REGRESSION: Review defeated the revision-2 teardown. The deferred pool rebuild waited for MLX's process-global `activeMemory` to fall below **half** its condemnation-time reading, then discarded the timeout and cleared unconditionally. Three defects in one gate: the threshold is proportional, so at the 29 GB target model it admits the clear with ~14.5 GB still active and still about to fall into the pool; the signal is process-global, so an unrelated allocation anywhere in the process satisfies it without one condemned buffer having been freed; and `waitForWeightRelease` returned `false` on timeout into a caller that ignored the Boolean, cleared the pending flag, performed one `Memory.clearCache()` and attested a completed rebuild.
- ROOT CAUSE: the **absent evidence treated as satisfied** shape. Failure to observe the release took the same success transition as observing it, so the ledger could report a rebuild that had returned nothing while the condemned model went on holding its buffers.
- FIX: the heuristic is gone, not tuned. `WeightReleaseBarrier` holds a `weak` reference to the exact `ModelContainer` the condemned engine was serving from and waits for it to read `nil` — ARC having destroyed *this* worker's weights. There is no threshold and no intermediate state: the object is alive or it has been destroyed. The half-baseline crossing, and the partial-release class it admitted, are eliminated by construction rather than bounded.
- FIX: the wait now fails closed. `GenerationBatchRecovery.teardownOutcome(releaseObserved:attempt:maxAttempts:)` is a pure gate that cannot reach `.rebuilt` without an observed release; unobserved attempts return `.retry` up to `workerTeardownAttempts` (3) and then `.abandoned`. An abandoned teardown performs no clear, attests no rebuild, leaves `shared_cache_rebuild_pending` raised and `shared_cache_rebuilds` at 0, counts `shared_cache_rebuilds_abandoned`, emits `generation_shared_cache_rebuild_abandoned` with `release_observed=false`, and re-announces the supervision marker so a host that could not return a condemned model is not left competing with its replacement for it.
- FINDING: an acceptance seam that lets go is not a seam. The first cut of `--fault-inject-teardown-retain` held the container only for the duration of the teardown wait; by the time the suite read the allocator the reference had been dropped and the 262 MB residue had already drained into `buffer_cache_`, so the phase measured `active_bytes=2,720` and failed its own residue floor. The seam now parks the container for the lifetime of the process — what is being reproduced is a reference that is genuinely stuck, so it gets genuinely stuck.
- EVIDENCE: the two teardown paths now separate cleanly on MLX's own figures — release observed: `active_bytes=2,720`, `cache_bytes=0`, `shared_cache_rebuilds=1`, pending `false`; release never observed: `active_bytes=262,361,760`, `shared_cache_rebuilds=0`, abandoned `1`, pending `true`. 98 checks exit 0; contract suite 169 tests exit 0; `dead-generation-smoke.sh` still 45 checks exit 0; lint and shellcheck clean.
- EVIDENCE: five mutants die. Contract — teardown always `.rebuilt` (the revision-2 shape) 7 issues; **narrowing**, fail closed while retrying but clear anyway at the bound, 3 issues; abandonment also attesting a rebuild, 4 issues. Production — the revision-2 shape end to end reddens **only** phase 6c with 6 failures at `active_bytes=262,361,760` beside `cache_bytes=0`, the defect measured directly; a barrier whose `isReleased` always returns `true` fails 7 checks and also reddens the 6a ordering gate at 303,848,400 B. Reviewer mutant M8 still dies after the restructure.
- STATUS: `TASK-260827-2q77g8` revision 3 handed back to review.

### 0130 — The Counter Is Not The Action, And The Pool Cannot Fix A Limit
- REGRESSION: Review defeated the generation-batch recovery suite shipped at 2325. Two production mutants left all 63 checks green. Deleting the only production `Memory.clearCache()` call kept `shared_cache_rebuilds=1` and the `generation_batch_released` event intact while MLX went on holding 67,955,820 cache bytes. Retaining each failed `ChatSession` kept the ledger reporting `active=0, batches_released=N` while the allocator gained 25,165,824 B of KV per failure. Both are the same defect: every resource claim in the suite was read back from a counter the ledger minted beside the action rather than from the action.
- ROOT CAUSE: `GenerationBatchLedger.fail` incremented `sharedCacheRebuilds` when it built the plan, not when anything was cleared, and the smoke asserted the ledger's own JSON. Self-minted evidence — the check and the thing checked had the same author.
- FIX: The counter moved to `recordSharedCacheRebuild()`, called one line after `Memory.clearCache()` returns and nowhere else. The suite now anchors every resource claim to `mlx.active_bytes` / `mlx.cache_bytes` from `Memory.snapshot()`. New leak phase: active bytes must not grow across failed generations. Rebuild phase measures cache bytes against a no-rebuild control run with identical model, prompt and pinned seed, so only the clear differs. Both reviewer mutants now redden the suite, at the reviewer's own numbers.
- REGRESSION: The condemned path returned nothing at all. Review measured 299,129,824 cache bytes on a condemned runtime whose ledger reported the pool rebuilt.
- ROOT CAUSE, and it took three attempts to place correctly: the clear ran while the weights were still held. Clearing inside the failing request is too early — the request holds the engine. Clearing after the response is written is *also* too early: MLX reported 303,782,980 active bytes at that point. Even `GenerationEngine.deinit` is too early, because a `deinit` body runs before its stored properties are released. The pool was emptied, and then the entire model fell into it.
- FIX: The teardown waits on an observed fact instead of an assumed ordering. `deinit` schedules a task that polls `Memory.snapshot().activeMemory` until it drops below half the condemnation-time reading — bounded to 2 s, and a timeout is reported rather than hidden — and only then clears. Measured after: `cache_bytes_before=329,274,476`, `cache_bytes=0`, `active_bytes=2,720`. The whole model goes back to the system. An ORDERING GATE in the suite fails if a condemned runtime is left holding it, so the ordering is checked rather than trusted.
- FINDING: `metal-allocation-oversize` was never shared-cache pressure, and the suite could not tell. `MetalAllocator::malloc` throws `Attempting to allocate N bytes which is greater than the maximum allowed buffer size` as its first act, before `std::unique_lock lk(mutex_)` and before any cache is consulted, testing `size > device_->maxBufferLength()`. `clear_cache()` empties `buffer_cache_` and cannot move `maxBufferLength()`, so no rebuild makes that request succeed. The 2325 entry's claim that the pool holds "freed-but-unreturned buffers that the next allocation of that size has to get past" is wrong for this throw and is corrected here. The old phase passed only because the request it made afterwards was small enough to succeed either way.
- FIX: The pressure class is now `metal-buffer-allocation-failed` — `[malloc]` **and** `Unable to allocate`, from the throw taken when `newBuffer` returns null. Reaching it means the allocator already ran `release_cached_buffers(mem_required - gc_limit_)`, a *slice* sized to get back under `gc_limit_`, and still failed; `clear_cache()` empties the pool outright, so it can return what that partial reclaim kept. `[malloc]` is not a substring of `[metal::malloc]`, so the pressure and condemning classes cannot collide through their tags. Oversize is now carried as a narrowing negative in both the unit suite and the smoke.
- EVIDENCE: 81 checks exit 0; contract suite 154 tests exit 0; `dead-generation-smoke.sh` still 45 checks exit 0. Reviewer mutant M8 (delete `Memory.clearCache()`) now fails the rebuild and ordering gates; M9 (retain the failed `ChatSession`) now fails the leak gate at +25,165,824 B and +50,331,648 B — the reviewer's exact measurements.
- STATUS: `TASK-260827-2q77g8` revision 2 handed back to review.

## 2026-08-27

### 2325 — Releasing A Failed Batch Is Not The Same As Surviving One
- DECISION: `mlx_lm.server`'s generation regression has a second half nobody had carried across. `TASK-260827-2h39ya` pinned the terminal case — a backend that is gone must stop answering `/health` 200. The common case is the opposite: a generation that fails mid-batch on a runtime still able to serve. In Python that was equally unsurvivable (the exception escaped the batch loop and killed the thread); in Swift the error propagates to its caller, so the runtime *can* recover, and now does so deliberately rather than by hoping ARC reaches the session before the next request needs the memory.
- FINDING: The prototype could not demonstrate recovery at all, for reasons that were all in the test seam rather than the runtime. `--fault-inject-generation-error` failed *every* attempt, so "a later request succeeds" was unprovable by construction; and it threw *before* any `ChatSession` existed, so nothing had been allocated and "releases invalid batch state" was unprovable too — a runtime that leaked every KV cache it ever built would have passed a check written that way. A seam that cannot be bounded or positioned is not a seam.
- FIX: New `GenerationBatchRecovery` splits recovery into two independent facts. The batch entry and its KV cache are released under *every* verdict, including a condemned one — the supervisor is about to start a replacement that needs the host's memory. MLX's *shared* buffer pool is dropped only when the failure implicates it (`metal-allocation-oversize`, conjunctive on `[metal::malloc]` **and** `Attempting to allocate`, from `allocator.cpp:111-117`). Collapsing the two would force a choice between never returning the pool, so an allocation failure repeats forever, and always returning it, so one malformed request throws away the pool that makes every other request fast.
- FINDING: The readiness transition must keep exactly one owner. The plan reports a condemning signature but never acts on it, so recovery cannot resurrect a worker `GenerationWorkerHealth` condemned. Phase 6a proves it: the injection has budget for exactly one fault, so a runtime that merely rearmed its engine would serve the next request — it must be refused 503 instead.
- FINDING: The ledger has to outlive the engine. Condemnation drops the engine, and "did the dead worker leak its batch" is a question only asked after there is no engine left; a ledger stored inside it would answer by disappearing, which reads exactly like a clean runtime. `GenerationBatchLedgerStore` is process-scoped, and `GET /debug/generation-state` answers 200 even once condemned. `active` is the only non-monotonic counter, and `batches_released` is tracked separately from `failed` rather than derived — deriving it would make the invariant unfalsifiable.
- FINDING: A token threshold above 1 makes the suite depend on how chatty the model feels. At `--after-tokens 3` two phases silently did not inject, because this 0.5B chat model answered the prompt in a single token; both failed loudly (200 where 500 was demanded) but for a reason with nothing to do with the runtime. Fixed at 1 — by the first chunk the whole prompt is already in the KV cache — plus a pinned `seed` so what the runtime is asked to recover from does not vary run to run.
- FINDING: The supervisor race from 2205 recurred exactly, and was rediscovered as six `000` connection-refused results: with `fatal_output_substrings` attached, `model-harness` kills the runtime within milliseconds of the marker and destroys the 503 window. The condemnation phase is split unsupervised/supervised for the same reason `dead-generation-smoke.sh` splits its own. Worth expecting in any future phase that wants to observe a condemned runtime.
- EVIDENCE: `scripts/generation-batch-recovery-smoke.sh`, 63 checks, exit 0, on the real Release binary under the real `model-harness`. Contract suite 151 tests exit 0; `dead-generation-smoke.sh` still 45 checks exit 0, so the 2h39ya contract is intact. `swift format lint` exit 0; the script is shellcheck-clean at warning severity, with the same info-level findings every sibling smoke carries.
- EVIDENCE: Six mutants, each reverted and the suite reconfirmed green afterwards. Five are unit-visible and redden `swift test` (exit 1): always-rebuild, never-rebuild, a disjunctive fragment matcher, a failed generation that never records its release, and a slot allowed to close twice. The always-rebuild mutant reddens *only* the narrowing expectations, which is what a narrowing gate should do.
- FINDING: The sixth mutant is the load-bearing one, and it is the reason the smoke has to exist. Deleting the ledger calls from `GenerationEngine.generate` — leaving the ledger type itself perfect and simply never called — keeps `swift test` at **exit 0, all 151 green**. The smoke catches it with 14 failures across every phase. A unit suite cannot tell a correct helper from an uncalled one; only driving the production entry point can.
- FINDING: A status-code assertion would have missed the truncated-success mutant. Making `generate` swallow the failure instead of rethrowing still answered the provoking request `500` — a downstream guard caught the missing completion info — so the HTTP code alone looked correct. What caught it was the body assertion that the error *names the injected failure*, plus the condemnation phase, which went `/health` 200 and served the next request from a worker that should have been condemned. Swallowing a generation error does not just lose an error; it silently repeals the 2h39ya contract.
- STATUS: `TASK-260827-2q77g8` handed to review. Python `mlx-lm` remains the default local runtime; nothing installed changed.

### 2242 — A Refusal Boundary Wide Enough To Catch Its Own Neighbour
- REGRESSION: Review defeated the dead-generation health gate shipped at 2205. `GenerationWorkerHealth.invalidatingSignatures` listed `metal::malloc` and `Resource limit` as two *independent* substrings, so `RequestError: Resource limit for this request is 8 tokens` — a request-scoped refusal that shares two words with the incident and nothing else — condemned a healthy runtime through the production entry point: `/health` and `/v1/models` to 503, engine dropped, fatal marker emitted, supervised restart. The cure fired on the disease it was supposed to distinguish itself from.
- ROOT CAUSE: The incident evidence is one message that asserts *two* facts at once — that MLX's Metal allocator produced it, and that the allocator is exhausted. Splitting it into independent OR-matched fragments kept the positive case working and silently widened the fatal class to every message carrying either half.
- FIX: Signatures are conjunctive. `BackendFailureSignature` requires *all* of its fragments; `metal-allocator-resource-limit` needs `[metal::malloc]` **and** `Resource limit`. The bracketed tag is not a guess — `mlx/backend/metal/allocator.cpp:141-144` in the vendored MLX emits `[metal::malloc] Resource limit (N) exceeded.` verbatim. An empty fragment list matches nothing rather than everything, because `allSatisfy` is vacuously true and a signature that lost its fragments would otherwise become a condemn-all gate by accident.
- FINDING: The same allocator's other throw (`allocator.cpp:111-117`, `[metal::malloc] Attempting to allocate N bytes which is greater than the maximum allowed buffer size`) is deliberately *not* fatal. It rejects one oversized allocation and leaves the pool intact, so it belongs to the request that asked for it. Carrying the allocator tag is not on its own proof that the backend is gone.
- FINDING: The shipped negative coverage was the real defect. Revision 1's benign case shared no words with any fatal signature, so it proved only that the gate is not condemn-all — it could not tell a correctly narrowed gate from a deleted one or an over-broad one. A negative case has to sit next to the boundary, not somewhere in the safe interior.
- EVIDENCE: `scripts/dead-generation-smoke.sh` gains phase 4b, injecting the reviewer's exact message. Rebuilding Release with the bare `Resource limit` signature restored reproduces the finding at the production entry point — 7 failures, all inside 4b, every other phase still green — with the runtime emitting `generation_worker_failed` for a request-scoped error and `model-harness` restarting it twice. Fixed build: 45 checks exit 0, contract suite 119 tests exit 0, three classifier mutants (widened, over-narrowed, empty-fragment guard deleted) each redden it.
- STATUS: `TASK-260827-2h39ya` reworked and handed back to review.

### 2205 — A Dead Generation Worker Must Not Answer /health 200, In Any Runtime
- DECISION: `mlx_lm.server`'s dead-generation-thread regression (`BUG-260827-1jhv2g`, fixed upstream in `BUG-260827-2tul5n`) does not port literally to the MLX Swift runtime, because Swift has no equivalent failure: an error thrown inside a structured task propagates to its caller instead of silently killing a worker. Carried across as **invalidation** rather than death — the runtime condemns itself when a generation fails with a signature that names the backend, not the request.
- FINDING: The Swift prototype had the same observable bug for a different reason. `Router.complete` caught a generation error, answered 500, and left readiness at `.ready`; `/health` kept answering `200 {"status":"ok"}` for a runtime whose backend was gone. Deleting the single line that now reports the failure reproduces the original incident exactly, and reddens 11 checks in `scripts/dead-generation-smoke.sh`.
- FIX: New `Sources/MLXSwiftRuntimeContract/GenerationWorkerHealth.swift` classifies the failure and owns the readiness transition; `HealthReport` owns the endpoint. `RuntimeReadiness` gained `.generationWorkerFailed`, so `/v1/models` — which is what the managed launcher actually polls — stops advertising too, under its own code rather than as a load failure. Production call sites: `RuntimeState.recordGenerationFailure`, `Router.health`, `Router.complete` and `RuntimeHTTPHandler.sendStream`.
- FINDING: The two completion paths are separate call sites into the same health state. Wiring only the buffered one would leave `/health` answering 200 for exactly the path Pi streams from; the smoke drives both.
- FINDING: The signature list is the whole gate, and it is dangerous in both directions. Condemning on every generation error is a *worse* bug than the one being fixed — one malformed request would take a healthy runtime out of rotation and, under supervision, restart it. Three signatures only, each from a recorded incident. Also only a `ready` runtime transitions: a generation killed by `SIGTERM` must not be reported as a dead worker and must not ask the harness to restart a process that was asked to stop.
- FINDING: Health-503 and supervised restart cannot be observed in one process. With `fatal_output_substrings` attached, `model-harness` kills the runtime within milliseconds of the marker reaching stdout — correct, and it destroys the 503 window. The smoke runs the same injected fault twice, unsupervised then supervised, so each phase measures one property instead of measuring which won the race.
- FINDING: `model-harness` does not forward signals to the runtime it spawns. Signalling the harness alone orphans a live listener; an early revision of the smoke then "passed" three phases against the leftover control process. Teardown now signals the process group and every phase refuses to start on an occupied port.
- EVIDENCE: `scripts/dead-generation-smoke.sh`, 35 checks, exit 0, on the real Release binary under the real `model-harness`. Uses the cached 261 MB `mlx-community/Qwen1.5-0.5B-Chat-4bit` — the failure path never reaches the weights, so paying 29 GB would only mean the check cannot run while the Python runtime is resident. Contract suite 116 tests exit 0; three mutants on the classifier (condemn-all, narrowed, health-returns-200) each redden it.
- STATUS: Python `mlx-lm` remains the default local runtime. `examples/model-harness.prototype.toml` gains the supervision policy but is still an example, not an installed config.

### 2115 — A Test That Cannot Fail Is Not Evidence
- ANOMALY: A new negative test claimed to prove that an unreadable model-directory *entry* is not reported as missing, but it chmod'ed the directory itself to `0o000`, which makes `contentsOfDirectory` fail first — the per-entry probe was never reached, and the test passed under a mutant that removed the very distinction it named.
- FIX: Rewritten to symlink a required file into a `0o000` subdirectory, keeping the directory listable while stat'ing the entry fails, and to assert the listing is still readable so the precondition itself is checked.
- NOTE: Surfaced only because a partial mutant was run and did not fail the test that should have owned it. Mutants that pass are worth as much as mutants that fail.

### 2110 — Existence Is Not Usability: Two Gates Minted Their Own Evidence
- REGRESSION: An independent review defeated two admission paths in the MLX Swift prototype. `MetalShaderLibraryCheck.inspect` used `FileManager.fileExists`, which answers true for a directory, so `mkdir default.metallib` passed the startup gate and the runtime bound its port on a build that could never reach the GPU. `ModelDirectoryCheck.observe` had the identical shape one level up: it matched required files by name in a directory listing, so `mkdir config.json` passed admission.
- ROOT CAUSE: Both gates asked "does something exist at this path" when the property they needed was "is there a loadable regular file here". The object the gate accepted as proof could be created by the thing being gated — forged evidence.
- FIX: New `Sources/MLXSwiftRuntimeContract/FileObjectProbe.swift` uses POSIX `stat` rather than Foundation, because the distinctions that matter are the ones Foundation blurs: `stat` follows symlinks so a link to a real file is admitted and a dangling one is not, and its `errno` separates `ENOENT` (proven absence) from `EACCES` (never absence). Both gates now route through it and name what they actually found.
- EVIDENCE: `scripts/metallib-gate-probe.sh` drives the composed executable, not a helper — a `swift build` product staged beside a forged object must exit 2 with no listener ever bound, and a real file at the same path must be admitted so a blanket-refusing gate cannot pass. Six narrowing mutants, all caught; under the existence-only mutant the probe reproduces the reviewer's observation exactly (listener bound on 28117).
- NOTE: `swift build` remains the build whose product lacks the shader library, which is precisely what makes this gate observable at the entry point at all.

### 2105 — An Out-Of-Range JSON Number Killed The Runtime, Bypassing Every Refusal
- REGRESSION: `{"max_tokens": 1e300}` terminated the whole managed runtime. A JSON number too large for `Int` decodes as a `Double`, and `ChatCompletionRequest.optionalInt` converted it with `Int(_:)` — a Swift fatal error, not a thrown one. Small invalid values reached the `invalidMaxTokens` refusal; a larger one killed the process that was supposed to issue it.
- FIX: The conversion is total — `Int(exactly:)` with a new `numberOutOfRange` decoding error, distinct from `wrongType` so an unrepresentable value and a fractional one do not share a message. The router already maps a decoding throw to `400 invalid_body`, so no other plumbing changed.
- EVIDENCE: Probed against the live ready runtime serving the real 28 GB model, each refusal followed by a health check — a bounded 400 from a process that then dies proves nothing. `1e300`, `-1e300` and `2^63` all return 400 and leave the runtime healthy; `max_tokens: 0` still returns `invalid_max_tokens`, proving the range gate rejects unrepresentable values rather than merely large ones. Now part of `scripts/smoke.sh`.
- NOTE: Reverting to `Int(_:)` kills the test process with signal 5, reproducing the reviewer's exit 133 on demand.

### 2012 — resident_bytes Is Not A Usable Memory Measure For MLX
- ANOMALY: Across three identical full-model loads `model_loaded.resident_bytes` read 14,056.9 / 10,774.2 / 2,650.2 MiB, while `physical_footprint_bytes` stayed within 16 MiB of 28,261 MiB and `mlx_active_bytes` was byte-identical at 29,501,612,496.
- FINDING: MLX weight buffers live in unified memory that `task_info` `resident_size` does not account for consistently. Size a host from the physical footprint or MLX active bytes, never from the resident figure.
- STATUS: Recorded as a measurement gap, not worked around; the runtime still reports all three.

### 2010 — DerivedData Repeats The .build Install-Sync Hazard
- REGRESSION: Building the prototype the only way that works (`xcodebuild -derivedDataPath ./DerivedData`) writes a 2.6 GB tree into the source directory, and `shouldSkip` excluded `.build` but not `DerivedData` — the same failure that took down `setup local` at 1453.
- FIX: `DerivedData` joins `.build`/`.temp` as a skipped path component in `tools/agents-infra/internal/infra/infra.go:467`; two tests in `internal/infra/source_sync_build_artifacts_test.go`, one for the exclusion and one pinning it to a whole-component match. `DerivedData/` added to the prototype `.gitignore`.
- NOTE: Both mutants caught — deleting the exclusion, and narrowing `hasPathComponent` to `strings.Contains`.

### 1958 — Full-Model MLX Swift Smoke Green; reasoning Key, Not reasoning_content
- MILESTONE: End-to-end smoke against the real 28 GB Qwen model passed with 0 failures through `model-harness run`: load 6.225 s, resident 10,774.2 MiB, physical footprint 28,261.7 MiB, MLX active/peak 29,501,612,496 B; first 503 at 2 s, ready at 8 s.
- FINDING: Non-streaming, streaming (35 frames / 34 chunks, one finish frame, one usage frame, `[DONE]`, no `</think>` leak) and tool-call (`finish_reason=tool_calls`, well-formed `tool_calls` with `call_...` id) all satisfy the contract the Pi profile uses.
- FINDING: Reasoning is published as `choices[].message.reasoning` and the key `reasoning_content` is absent, so a generic OpenAI client expecting `reasoning_content` sees nothing. Deliberate parity with `mlx_lm.server`; recorded as a named gap, not a bug.
- STATUS: Python `mlx-lm` remains the default local runtime and rollback path; no installed config was changed.

### 1957 — mlx-swift Cannot Be Built From The SwiftPM Command Line
- ROOT CAUSE: `swift build` produces an MLX binary with no Metal shader library, so MLX aborts with `Failed to load the default metallib` (4 failed lookups) at the first GPU touch — for this runtime, halfway through a multi-GiB weight load, after the port is already bound. mlx-swift's README says it outright: "SwiftPM (command line) cannot build the Metal shaders so the ultimate build has to be done via Xcode."
- FINDING: In the pinned 0.31.6 checkout `Cmlx` declares no `resources:`, no `.metallib` exists anywhere under `.build`, and the `PrepareMetalShaders` step referenced at `Package.swift:265` no longer ships. `Package.swift:202-203` defines `SWIFTPM_BUNDLE="mlx-swift_Cmlx"` / `METAL_PATH="default.metallib"`, which `backend/metal/device.cpp` resolves as `<root>/mlx-swift_Cmlx.bundle/Contents/Resources/default.metallib`.
- FINDING: An unattended `xcodebuild` needs BOTH `-skipPackagePluginValidation` (mlx-swift's `CudaBuild` plugin) and `-skipMacroValidation` (mlx-swift-lm's `MLXHuggingFaceMacros`); each otherwise fails the build outright. The Metal Toolchain is a separate Xcode component — `xcodebuild -downloadComponent MetalToolchain`, ~688 MB — without which the shader compile fails with `cannot execute tool 'metal'`.
- FIX: Build path corrected in `tools/mlx-swift-runtime-prototype/README.md`; new startup gate `Sources/MLXSwiftRuntimeContract/MetalShaderLibraryCheck.swift`, admitted in `Sources/mlx-swift-runtime-prototype/Main.swift` before the listener binds and reported as a `metal_shader_library` stage by `Preflight.swift`.
- DECISION: The gate treats an unreadable search root as `.undetermined`, not as absence, and does not refuse the launch on it — failing to read a directory is not evidence the library is gone.
- EVIDENCE: The surviving `swift build` product now exits 2 with an actionable message and never binds the port; previously that same command bound the port and then aborted mid-load.

### 1455 — MLX Swift LM Already Implements This Qwen Architecture
- FINDING: `mlx-swift-lm` 3.31.4 registers `qwen3_5` in both `MLXVLM.VLMTypeRegistry` and `MLXLLM.LLMTypeRegistry`, and the local model's own `config.json` decodes into `MLXVLM.Qwen35Configuration`; because the model carries a `vision_config`, the VLM factory is the one that claims it.
- FINDING: swift-transformers 1.3.3 loads the tokenizer and swift-jinja 2.4.2 renders the chat template including tool declarations; tool-call format is inferred as `xml_function`, matching the template's `<tool_call><function=...>` shape.
- EVIDENCE: `mlx-swift-runtime-prototype preflight` exit 0 on `/Users/alexis/src/local-models/Qwen3.8-27B-Uncensored-MLX-8bit`, at mlx-swift `0bb916c67f4b9e5c682cbe02a42c701c93ab5021` / mlx-swift-lm `bd4b7434e6bdb588c7ef55706ff8904cb7fd4c57`. There is no unsupported-architecture gap for this model; the open questions are runtime behaviour, not compatibility.

### 1454 — Swift Has No Reasoning Split, Python Does
- FINDING: `MLXLMCommon.Generation` has exactly three cases — `chunk`, `info`, `toolCall`. Nothing in `MLXLMCommon` handles thinking or reasoning, so an OpenAI-compatible Swift runtime must implement the `<think>` split itself; Python gets it free from `mlx_lm.generate.TextStateMachine`.
- FINDING: The Qwen3.5 template ends its generation prompt with a bare `<think>\n`, so generation starts *inside* the reasoning block and the model emits only the closing `</think>`. Verified empirically by preflight, not assumed.
- DECISION: The prototype publishes reasoning under the key `reasoning`, matching `mlx_lm.server`'s `generate_response`, not the `reasoning_content` some other OpenAI-compatible servers use. Parity with the runtime the Pi profile is configured against beats generic-client convention.

### 1453 — SwiftPM .build Broke The Install Sync
- ROOT CAUSE: `shouldSkip` in `tools/agents-infra/internal/infra/infra.go` picks installable files from an explicit deny-list of path components and never reads `.gitignore`. Once the new SwiftPM package was compiled, `setup local` tried to copy `tools/mlx-swift-runtime-prototype/.build/`, and a mode-0400 `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` inside a swift-crypto bundle failed the copy with `permission denied`, taking the whole run down.
- FIX: `.build` joins `.temp` as a skipped path component in `infra.go:467`; coverage in `internal/infra/source_sync_build_artifacts_test.go`.
- EVIDENCE: `TestInstalledBinarySetupLocalPiInfraRepairsModeAndSymlinkDrift` failed before the fix and `go test ./... -count=1` exits 0 after it. Two mutants caught: removing the exclusion, and widening it to `strings.Contains(rel, "build")` — the latter matters because a substring match would also swallow `tools/build/main.go` and `tools/.buildkite/`.

### 1452 — One 27B Model Per 64GiB Host
- BLOCKED: The full-model load and generation smokes for `TASK-260827-qyebv8` could not run. The model needs ~27.5 GiB resident, mostly wired by MLX, and the host already held one copy: Python `mlx_lm.server` pid 78542 at 23.1 GiB RSS, system wired 40–43 GiB, free+inactive 5.6–10.7 GiB across five samples.
- FINDING: `agents-infra runtime status` showed the shared broker serving one lease in state `held`, heartbeating every 1.8–4.7 s, owned by pid 12116 — the operator's own interactive `agents-infra target qwen-infra -- --continue` session in a zellij pane, live for over 90 minutes. Not stopped.
- DECISION: Everything that does not need the weights resident was proven instead — a `preflight` subcommand for architecture/config/tokenizer/template, and a lifecycle smoke driven by a fixture no factory can load. Both cost zero GPU memory. The full smoke script is written and ready; it needs a host not already serving this model.

### 0230 — Verify A Mutant By Narrowing It, Not By Reading Its Row
- FINDING: The F1 closure was confirmed by re-introducing the defect, not by inspecting the fix. Reproducing the pre-repair wiring inside the delivered tree — run the specified gate on the delivered bytes, then evaluate the mutant against a constant harness-built plain frame — reddens all three new `TestSharedRuntimeShapeMutantsDriveProductionLauncherGate` subtests. Withdrawing `reject_all_probe` from the `TestMain` entry allowlist reddens its production-entry test with `unknown shape mutant`. The delivered regressions are discriminating.
- FINDING: The sharpest measurement was a probe mutant, `probe_unsupplied_dimension`, deciding on `frame.dimension` — a quantity the production decode path never supplies, i.e. the `RUN-260825-86b7d5` F1 shape verbatim. Under the delivered calibration it fails as `coverage hole: corpus kills no frame`. Under the pre-repair routing restored, the same mutant is reported `KILLED … admits=340`. A predicate production never evaluates, credited with 340 kills.
- FINDING: 340 is the second fabricated kill count this contract has produced, after the 48 that hid the reject-all mutant in revision 8. Both were large. The general lesson holds and is worth carrying: **a large witness count is not evidence of a mutant's health — it is the signature a broken mutant produces.** What discriminates is where the mutant's input comes from, not how many frames it disagrees on.
- FINDING: The gate itself survived 22 raw forged frames driven byte-wise through the real `runtime-launch` entry, each refusal carrying a never-exec proof. The two rows that matter beyond the existing table: a duplicate whose second value is **wrong** still refuses `frame_duplicate_field`, and an unknown member alongside a **wrong** `protocol_version` still refuses `frame_unknown_field` — §6.2 B12 step 4 genuinely precedes step 5, so the shape gate is not reachable only through value-correct frames.
- NOTE: `sharedAuthDecodeEvidence.DecisionCallSite` and `.ConstantFieldSet` are emitted as literals inside the decoder, so under any of the 18 mutants the record still names the compiled constant set while a different decision is installed. `DecodedKeys` and `ComparedFields` are measured; those two are not, and should not be cited as if they were. The §12.4 structural obligation rests on the measured pair plus the calibration.
- NOTE: `tokenizeSharedAuthorizationFrame` now runs the five value decodes before the trailing-content check. A frame that is both value-unparseable and has trailing content reports `frame_unparseable` where it once reported `frame_not_single_object`. No admitted frame changed, but the 398-frame corpus contains no such combination, so the differential would not have caught a real inversion there.
- STATUS: `CR-TASK-260825-lsojra-2` **accepted** by `RUN-260825-e2bfd4`. Measured at `8518d45`, four commits behind `main`; trunk moved inside the same package (`model_check.go`, `pi_config.go`, `pi_launch_posix.go`, `main.go`), so the green suites do **not** predict a trunk-green result and integration must re-run them on the merged tree.

### 2245 — A Mutation Harness's Controls Are Gates Too
- REGRESSION: The fifth review found the corpus, the oracle and the decoder sound, and one **mutant** not the mutant its row described. `unknown_by_wire_form` was documented as matching the allowlist against a member's wire spelling; it was wired through `isAllowedName(m.name, mutant)`, which rebuilds a `frameMember` from the decoded name and therefore clears `wire`. The empty wire string matched no allowed literal, so it refused **every member of every frame**. It was still reported KILLED — by 48 witnesses that were all ordinary *valid* frames beginning `arity/schema/x1` — and its `blind` label was a table boolean nothing executed.
- ROOT CAUSE: Revision 8's kill condition was `disagrees with the oracle on at least one frame`, and a reject-all fake disagrees on every accepted frame. The witness count was not just non-zero, it was large, and no rule was in a position to read that as the tell it was.
- FINDING: The general form, and it is the one worth carrying past this task: **a mutation harness's controls are gates, and the same evidence rule applies to them.** "The mutant was killed" is a positive result *about the harness*. Nothing in revision 8 would have failed if a mutant were the wrong mutant, which is exactly the shape this project refuses in product code.
- DECISION: Three normative obligations on every mutant (spec §12.3.1), all executed. (1) It must still **admit the plain valid frame** — every mutant narrows or deletes a *refusal* clause, so none can turn away a frame the specification admits; checked at the decoder and again at the production `runtime-launch` entry, one process per mutant. (2) It must **declare the side** it disagrees on and disagree only there; exactly one mutant in the table over-refuses and every other admits. (3) **Blindness is measured**, not declared: each mutant names the hand-written baselines it is green on — `rev6`, `rev7`, `review_c71188` — and the harness recomputes the truth from a 19-row inventory, failing on an over-claim **and** on an under-claim.
- DECISION: Prove the three rules can fail. `reject_all_probe` is the review's defect reproduced on purpose, is not a gate mutant, and the suite shows all three reddening on it. Without it §12.3.1 is positive-path-only evidence about the harness — the same defect one level up.
- FINDING: Rule 3 corrected the document on its first run, and by measurement rather than reasoning. Revision 8 claimed seven mutants blind to every hand-written row; the truth is **four**. The two mutants review `RUN-260825-c71188` inspired are not blind to that review's own rows — those rows are what caught them — and `dup_only_when_separated` is caught by `dup_same_exec_plan_digest`, because the same-value duplicate rows *append* the repeat and `exec_plan_digest` is the frame's **last** member, so that one row alone places the two occurrences adjacent.
- FINDING: The repaired mutant's witness count is **6**, not 48, and all six are on the *accepted* side: the five single-escaped frames and the all-escaped one. Forty-eight was the count of valid frames a reject-all fake turned away. Revision 8's own conclusion — a gate proved only by inputs it must reject cannot be shown to admit what it must admit — held; the number attached to it was measuring something else.
- DECISION: No protocol-version bump, and measured this time rather than asserted. `protocol_version` stays **6** for the fourth revision running: revision 8's wiring is retained under the name `rev8_unknown_wiring` and required to agree with revision 9's on all **417** frames (398 generated + 19 hand-written) in verdict, reason and named member, so §9 leaves no client a bump would protect.
- STATUS: `TASK-260825-imfmgz` revision 9 (`RUN-260825-fc8f60`) closes review `RUN-260825-86b7d5` F1 and its five rework items. Probe module rev9: **200 passes, 0 failures, 190.6s** uncached on darwin 26.5.1/arm64 go1.25.5, and `ok 191.5s` from a clean extract with `GOPROXY=off GOFLAGS=-mod=vendor`. `tools/agents-infra` builds, vets and passes all three packages as a **no-regression control**, not a test of the delta — the delta is the specification and this logbook. Probe modules rev2-rev6 were **not** rerun by this producer: they are untouched since revision 8, where they were reconstructed and rerun green, and the revision-9 delta is confined to the revision-9 module.

### 2115 — Stop Naming The Class; Differentiate Against An Oracle
- REGRESSION: The fourth review in a row defeated the same gate's *evidence*, one dimension further out. Revision 7 proved "exactly once" over all five members and "outside the closed five" over four near-miss name classes. Every one of those duplicate rows has arity **two**, and every one of those unknown names is at most **19 bytes** — so a decoder that refuses a repeat only at a total count of exactly two, and one that applies the allowlist only up to 32 bytes, keep the entire revision-7 table green and reach `execve` on a third occurrence and on a 33-byte member.
- ROOT CAUSE: Not an oversight, and this is the point. A table of frames-and-expected-verdicts requires its author to **name the admitted class in advance**; the reviewer only has to find one they did not name. Revisions 6 and 7 each answered the previous review by adding rows, and each lost to a dimension the new rows did not vary. Three revisions is enough evidence that the arrangement, not the diligence, is what fails.
- DECISION: State the rule **constructively** so it has one bound instead of three. B12 step 4 is now one decision — the decoded member-name multiset `M` is admitted iff `M` **equals** the compiled allowed set with multiplicity one — and the unknown/duplicate/missing clauses are demoted to a classification of *how* `M` differs, used only to populate `reason` and `mismatch_field`. Three clauses can be narrowed one at a time; an equality against a compiled constant cannot be narrowed without changing the constant. Normative consequence: the decision **may not be expressed as a predicate over any quantity derived from the frame** — no count literal, no length ceiling, no adjacency test, no folded or prefix match, no comparison against the bytes of a key.
- DECISION: Prove it by **differential against an independent oracle**, not by a table. The predicate is small enough to implement twice, so P23 implements it a second time — reading the generator's own record of what it built, never the bytes and never the decoder — and requires agreement on accept/refuse, on the refusal `reason` **and** on the named member, over a generated corpus of 398 frames sweeping seven dimensions. No expected verdict is written next to any frame. A narrowed gate is then caught because it **disagrees with the oracle**, not because someone predicted the class it admits.
- DECISION: Invert the mutant table. It is no longer the coverage claim; it is the harness's **calibration**. Each mutant must be killed *by the corpus*, the harness reports which generated frame killed it, and **a mutant nothing kills fails the suite** — a coverage hole reported by the harness rather than found by the next reviewer. Seven of eighteen are blind to every hand-written row, including the review's own two.
- FINDING: Self-found, and no corpus this task had ever built could have seen it. Nothing in revisions 6 or 7 said whether a member name is the **decoded** name or the bytes it was written as, and the two readings disagree: `{"\u0073chema": …}` is a valid frame under one and an unknown member under the other, and `"schema"` plus `"\u0073chema"` in one object is the same member twice under one and two members under the other. The wire-form mutant **admits nothing extra** — it is stricter — and it **refuses a valid frame**. It is caught only by frames on the *accepted* side, and every corpus before revision 8 was built exclusively from frames that must be refused. Decoded-name byte equality is now normative in both directions.
- FINDING: The general form, worth carrying past this task: **a gate proved only by inputs it must reject cannot be shown to admit what it must admit.** Its sibling from revision 7 — an attack a different gate happens to stop is not evidence for the gate under test — is the same mistake on the other side of the verdict.
- FINDING: The packaging defect the fourth review raised (F2: loose sources referencing a helper harness no attached resource defined, so the reviewer's rerun failed to compile) is **older than the revision it was raised against**. The attached revision-4 sources call `notifySignal`, defined in no attached revision-4 resource; that rerun is now labelled a reconstruction with the one supplied line stated. Evidence is attached as a whole module with vendored dependencies from revision 8 on, verified by extracting into an empty directory and running with `GOPROXY=off`.

### 2005 — A Rule Stated As A Class Cannot Be Proved By A Sample
- REGRESSION: Revision 6's decoder was correct and its **proof** did not bind anything to it. Section 12.2.30c stated two rules as classes — "each allowed key exactly once" and "any member outside the closed five" — then tested one duplicate value pair (`999` and the valid value, in both orders) and one unknown member name. The sixth review built two narrowed decoders that keep **every** revision-6 row green while admitting frames the specification refuses: refuse a duplicate only when its two decoded values *differ*, which admits `protocol_version` written twice with the same valid value; and refuse only the literal name `caller_chosen_field`, which admits `future_extension` and every other unknown member.
- ROOT CAUSE: A delete-only mutant proves a clause is *present*. It says nothing about the class the clause covers, so a narrowed implementation passes the whole table. The revision-6 table had six deletions and no narrowings for these two clauses.
- DECISION: Prove a finite closed set exhaustively and an open one by near-miss class. "Exactly once" quantifies over five members, so all five are now duplicated with **their own valid value**, one row each. "Outside the closed five" quantifies over an infinite name space, so it is covered by classes — an unrelated name, a case variant of an allowed name, an allowed name used as a prefix — each paired with a narrowing that only that class defeats: refuse-one-name, case-folded allowlist, prefix-matched allowlist. Five narrowings joined the six deletions.
- FINDING: The valid value in the duplicate rows is the whole point. A duplicate carrying a *wrong* value is refused by the equality comparison whether or not a duplicate clause exists — which is why a value-sensitive gate sails through every wrong-value row. The decoder must not consult the values at this step at all, and that is now normative in B12 step 4.
- FINDING: Measured, and it invalidated the case-variant row as first written. Go's `encoding/json` matches an object key to a struct field by exact tag first and by **case-folded name** second, so a member named `Schema` is not discarded like `future_extension` — it is absorbed into `schema` and overwrites it. A case variant carrying an arbitrary value therefore never reached `execve` under any narrowed unknown mutant: the equality comparison stopped it, and the clause under test was never exercised. It discriminates only when it carries `schema`'s own valid value. Both variants are now in the table, the wrong-value one labelled non-discriminating. Consequence, now normative: the shape gate runs on the member names **as they appeared in the bytes**, never on what a case-folding structure decode resolved them to.
- FINDING: This is revision 6's duplicate-order lesson one level down, and the general form is worth carrying past this task: **an attack that a different gate happens to stop is not evidence for the gate under test.** Both times the row was green, both times the reason had nothing to do with the clause it was written for.
- DECISION: No protocol-version bump, on the same rule that forced the last two. Revision 7 adds evidence and pins two existing rules as classes; the launcher's gate set is byte-identical, so there is no client a bump would protect. Bumping here would be as wrong as skipping it was in revisions 5 and 6.
- STATUS: `TASK-260825-imfmgz` revision 7 (`RUN-260825-34ba50`) closes review `RUN-260825-9d5cff` F1 and its five rework items. Probe module rev7: 109 passes, 0 failures on darwin 25.5.0/arm64 go1.25.5 — P22.G's five same-value duplicate rows, P22.H's four unknown-name classes, eleven mutants (six deletions, five narrowings) each reddening its own named rows, and P22.I rerunning the reviewer's two frames at the production entry point with the all-valid control still reaching `execve`. Probe modules rev2-rev6 rerun green. `tools/agents-infra` builds, vets and passes all three packages as a regression baseline — the delta is the specification and this logbook.

- REGRESSION: P22.H's five unknown-name rows are all at most 19 bytes. A narrowed unknown-member gate that rejects only names up to 32 bytes keeps every named row green and admits a 33-byte unknown member to `execve`.
